\- Sergey Brin***

Before the age of computers and the Internet, even before the age of written maps and magnetic compasses, there was a group of ingenious people traversing the Indian and Pacific oceans on small canoes with just their own mental abilities. They sailed from island to island, carrying with them their culture—their language, technology, and beliefs—and spreading it as far as Madagascar in the west and Easter Island in the east. These ocean-faring people have been identified to belong to a common group known as the Austronesians. \[HRS\]

![](Images/image00171.jpeg)

Figure 1.0 Migration of the Austronesian people.: \[AUS\]

If you look at the map of the world, Madagascar is a long way from Easter Island. If we draw a line from Madagascar to Easter Island passing through Southeast Asia, it’s about 13 thousand nautical miles. The Vikings crossed the Atlantic reaching Atlantic Canada on the west and European Russia on the east, which by comparison is a distance of about 2.7 thousand nautical miles. To help the Austronesians, they developed crude yet effective technologies: dugout or planked canoes with outriggers and sails, and created way-finding methods for navigation relying only on the scarce information they could observe in the vast ocean.\[BEL\]

Unfortunately, little of the original methods survived modernization, but what remains are being preserved by tribes and enthusiasts who keep the ingenuity and traditions of the ancient Austronesians alive. The original Austronesian boat designs can be found both in the islands of Madagascar and Easter Island and in the thousands of islands between them revealing how effective these simple designs were for ocean scale navigation.\[HRS\]

Way-finding is the term adopted by modern practitioners for the art of sailing the oceans with only the minimum of instruments. Way-finding is when a human has to maximize his observational skills and develop an intuition so as to reach a small island within the wide expanse of the ocean.

This, however, is not a book about human intuition, but rather a book of another kind of intuition. This is a book about technology created by humans. Specifically, it is about artificial intuition. Artificial intuition is technology navigating the oceans of information complexity to discover tiny islands of importance.

Coursera co-founder and Stanford adjunct professor Andrew Ng spoke recently about AI transforming industry:  
*AI is the new electricity. Electricity transformed industries: agriculture, transportation, communication, and manufacturing. I think we are now in that phase where AI technology has advanced to the point where we see a clear path for it to transform multiple industries. Just as electricity transformed industry after industry 100 years ago, I think AI will do the same.* \[EAD\]  
AI has been around since the 1950s and has gone through several boom and bust cycles. In the last five years, however, a new kind of AI called Deep Learning unexpectedly emerged. Since 2012, it has been delivering spectacular and surprising results. Many people see the progress but don’t realize the catalyst. Previous AI failures, the ambiguity regarding what AI means and the newness of this emerging technology are all making it difficult for people to comprehend what is occurring.  
This problem is further exacerbated by mass media that continues to dole out sensational articles exhibiting boom and gloom. The press either writes about capabilities outside the realm of what is feasible or a far-in-the-future scenario in the realm of science fiction. This is compounded with the constant drumbeat of spectacular research results coming from the academe and industry. Indeed, a lot of excitement is coupled with mass confusion.  
Deep Learning is an unexpected revolution in artificial intelligence. Google’s founder Sergey Brin was completely caught by surprise and this is a person who should be finally in tune with the latest computer science developments. I suspect 99% of the world’s population has never even heard of the term ‘Deep Learning.’ Even if they have, they likely have the slightest clue of what it is about.

### A New Kind of Artificial Intelligence

Peter Thiel uses the phrase, “The Last Company Advantage.” \[COM\] Although you don’t necessarily need to have the “First Mover Advantage,” you absolutely want to be the last company standing in your business. So Google may be the last Search company, Amazon may be the last ECommerce company, and Facebook may (but hopefully not) be the last Social Networking company. What keeps me awake at night though, is that Deep Learning could in fact be the “Last Invention of Man!”

Deep Learning is technology as revolutionary as the Internet and mobile computing that came before it.

The current revival of interest in all things “Artificial Intelligence” (AI) is primarily due to the spectacular results achieved with Deep Learning research. I must, however, emphasize that this revival is not due to other classical AI technologies like expert systems, semantic knowledge bases, logic programming, or Bayesian systems. Most of classical AI has not changed much in the last five years. The recent quantum leap is solely driven by Deep Learning successes. Many venture capitalists like to cling to the term AI, but these days, the term has become meaningless.\[BOG\]

For some perspective on the extent of Deep Learning development, look at this graph from Google showing the adoption of Deep Learning technology in their applications:

![](Images/image00172.jpeg)Figure 1.1 Growth of Deep Learning uses at Google. \[GIL\]

As you can see, the adoption at Google is exponential and the statistics are likely similar for many of the other big Internet firms like Facebook and Microsoft.

When Google embarked on converting their natural language translation software into using Deep Learning (DL), they were surprised to discover major gains. This is best described in a recent article published in the NY Times, “The Great AI Awakening” \[LEW\]:

The neural system, on the English-French language pair, showed an improvement over the old system of seven points. Hughes told Schuster’s team they hadn’t had even half as strong an improvement in their own system in the last four years.

To be sure this wasn’t some fluke in the metric , they also turned to their pool of human contractors to do a side-by-side comparison. The userperception scores, in which sample sentences were graded from zero to six, showed an average improvement of 0.4—roughly equivalent to the aggregate gains of the old system over its entire lifetime of development.

*In mid-March, Hughes sent his team an email. All projects on the old system were to be suspended immediately.* \[LEW\]  
Let’s pause to recognize what happened at Google.  
Since its inception, Google used every type of AI or machine learning technology imaginable. In spite of this, their average gain for improvement per year was only 0.4%. In Google’s first implementation, the improvement due to DL was seven percentage points better.  
This translates to more improvement than the entire lifetime of improvements combined!  
Google likely has the most talented AI and algorithm developers on the planet. Several years of handcrafted classical AI and algorithm development could not hold a candle against a single initial Deep Learning implementation.  
Deep Learning is unexpectedly, and disruptively, taking over the world!  
Google’s founder Sergey Brin, an extremely talented computer scientist himself, stated in a recent World Economic Forum \[CHA\] discussion that he did not foresee deep learning:  
*The revolution in deep nets has been very profound, it definitely surprised me, even though I was sitting right there.* \[CHA\]  
Sundar Pichai in a recent quarterly financial call said \[LEV\]:  
*Machine learning is a core, transformative way by which we’re re- thinking how we’re doing everything.* \[LEV\]  
The more precise term is “Deep Learning.” He just described it in terms the less educated financial press could perhaps comprehend.  
Deep Learning progress has been taking the academic community by storm. Two articles by practitioners of classical machine learning summarize why they think DL is taking over the world. Chris Manning, a renowned expert in NLP, writes about the “Deep Learning Tsunami” \[MAN\]:  
*Deep Learning waves have lapped at the shores of computational linguistics for several years now, but 2015 seems like the year when the full force of the tsunami hit the major Natural Language Processing (NLP) conferences. However, some pundits are predicting that the final damage will be even worse.* \[MAN\]  
The same sentiment is expressed by Nicholas Paragios, who works in the field of computer vision. Paragios writes in “Computer Vision Research: the Deep Depression” \[PARG\]  
*It might be simply because Deep Learning on highly complex, hugely determined in terms of degrees of freedom graphs once endowed with massive amount of annotated data and unthinkable—until very recently— computing power can solve all computer vision problems. If this is the case, well it is simply a matter of time that industry (which seems to be already the case) takes over, research in computer vision becomes a marginal academic objective and the field follows the path of computer graphics (in terms of activity and volume of academic research).*\[PARG\]  
Make no mistake--Deep Learning is a “Disruptive” technology taking over the operations of the most advanced technology companies in the world.  
Disruption happens when some new way of doing things entirely changes the way we think, act, learn and go about our daily activities. Clayton Christensen expresses this more specifically: disruption displaces an existing market, industry or technology; it replaces what exists today with something that is simply better.  
The Internet gave civilization a more efficient way to communicate. The World Wide Web introduced a better way to exchange knowledge. Mobile computing gave people ubiquitous access to communication and computation. Deep Learning enhances our cognitive capabilities by making it accessible through the technologies that came before it. Deep Learning is an enabling technology that will improve how we do things across every human activity. Mark Cuban remarked recently \[CLI\]:  
*“Whatever you are studying right now if you are not getting up to speed on deep learning, neural networks, etc., you lose,” says Cuban. “We are going through the process where the software will automate software, automation will automate automation.”* \[CLI\]

### What is Deep Learning?

Deep Learning, when separated, are two simple words we can all understand, but in the context of Artificial Intelligence, when these words are combined, they become inscrutable to the uninformed. In fact, even for the informed, it is inscrutable. That’s because decades worth of statistical training have become a liability in understanding what it means.

Deep Learning is a Connectionist approach to Artificial Intelligence. A Connectionist system is composed of a large number of simple components collectively exhibiting complex behavior. Deep Learning specifically employs multiple layers of components arranged in an acyclic graph (i.e. without loops.) There are many kinds of layers but their common property is that the layers are differentiable. Another way of saying this is, the gradient can be calculated for any layer. We will explore this structure in greater detail in a later chapter.

Here are nine points to address some misconceptions of Deep Learning and some explanations saying otherwise:  
1\. **Deep Learning is NOT Good Old Fashion AI (GOFAI)**

Expert systems, semantic web, and deductive logic systems are examples of systems based on symbolic logic. These systems are traditionally associated with AI. They all do work, but they do have one shortcoming: they are unable to learn effectively from data. GOFAI needs developers to handcraft symbolic rules.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT Machine Learning**

Machine Learning in its most basic distillation is “curve fitting .” That is, if you have an algorithm capable of finding the best fit of your mathematical model with observed data, then that’s Machine Learning. Although both ML and DL can learn from data, there are many capabilities existing in DL that doesn’t exist in ML. Examples of this difference support for transfer learning and incremental training.

1\. **Deep Learning does NOT mimic Biological Brains**

The architecture DL has is nowhere close to a biological neuron in structure. Even in behavior, they are different. Biological neurons work on spiking behavior, DL systems work as a continuous dynamical system. Some DL systems use Artificial Neural Networks, but this is just historic terminology still existing to this day. Anyone explaining DL in terms of biological neurons really don’t know what they are talking about. DL isn’t designed to ‘mimic’ biology, DL just happens to be a computational architecture that learns surprisingly well.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT Artificial General Intelligence**

DL can do some fantastic things like cross translate between different human languages and read out captions from images. The intelligence is however really specialized and narrow. Sure DL can drive cars, but that’s nowhere near the capability of AGI.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT “Just Math”**

There was a Wired article titled “Deep Learning isn’t a Dangerous Genie , it is Just Math” \[ETZ\]  and this is really the most vacuous statement I have ever heard! It is like saying computers are just boolean circuits, or that brains are just made up of neurons, or that DL is just made up of layers that are described using mathematical functions. It doesn’t explain the emergent complex behavior you’ll find in computers, brains and DL systems.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT Statistics**

Classical statistics is about analyzing data using aggregate measures. DL systems, however, work in a domain where statistical methods do not apply; it is high-dimensional data with high mutual information among the variables. Simplifying i.i.d. (Independent and identically distributed \[IID\]) assumptions are simply not applicable to many realistic scenarios.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT Big Data**

Big Data is a technology based on the idea that if you are able to store and compute through a massive amount of data, and typically hosted in hundreds or thousands of off-the-shelf computers, then you can gain insight. DL is an algorithm sitting on a single machine and can incrementally (special emphasis on incrementally) process your data to learn from it. Big Data can crunch massive amounts of data, but just because you can process a lot of data, doesn’t mean you can derive insight or learn from the data. One last point: unlike Big Data, DL doesn’t need a lot of data to be useful.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT understood by Data Scientists**

Data Scientists are trained to do modeling of data, feature engineering, and data analysis. DL does what a Data Scientist does, but without a human in the loop. This is actually a bit of an exaggeration. The reality is most Data Scientists trained in other methods have not come up to speed with DL techniques.

1\. **Deep Learning is NOT just Artificial Neural Networks (ANN) or Multi-Level Perceptrons (MLP)**

ANN or MLPs were developed way back in the 1950s. There is a common misconception Deep Learning today is no different from the older approach. However in recent years, new kinds of layer models such as Convolution layers, Autoregressive layers, Long Short Term Memory and Residual layers have extended the original approach. The field has a much richer collection of concepts now than when you first studied it in graduate school.

One way to look at the correspondence of AI, GOFAI, Machine Learning, and Deep Learning is through an evolutionary tree. Different approaches emerged at different times and over time, new approaches emerged. So in 1955, the term Artificial Intelligence was coined. The first approach that dominated the space was the GOFAI (i.e. Symbolist). This can be analogous to the emergence of plants. With the development of statistical methods like Bayesian-based graph models and Machine Learning techniques, a new kind of approach arrived capable if adapting to data. Consider these as the emergence of reptiles. Deep Learning came into prominence, but the ideas were around since the late 1950s. Let’s make the analogy, these are like mammals, evolving from reptiles with distinctly new capabilities. So when many writers make the statement that Deep Learning is Machine Learning, it is as nonsensical as saying Mammals are Reptiles. Similarly, GOFAI is not Deep Learning, but rather like plants turned into tools for mammals, the correspondence of GOFAI with Deep Learning, is analogous with paper and humans. Paper is a way for humans to write down knowledge. Symbolist approaches are ways to encode knowledge.

### Tribes of Artificial Intelligence

One of the biggest confusions about “Artificial Intelligence” is its very vague term. That’s because Artificial Intelligence or AI is a term coined way back in 1955 with extreme hubris:

We propose that a 2 month, 10 man study of artificial intelligence be carried out during the summer of 1956 at Dartmouth College in Hanover, New Hampshire.

The study is to proceed on the basis of the conjecture that every aspect of learning or any other feature of intelligence can in principle be so precisely described that a machine can be made to simulate it. An attempt will be made to find how to make machines use language, form abstractions, and concepts, solve kinds of problems now reserved for humans, and improve themselves.

*Dartmouth AI Project Proposal; J. McCarthy et al.; Aug. 31, 1955.* AI is over half a century old and carries too much baggage with it. For a very long time, Symbolists dominated AI, a rulebased system with “Zero Learning.” In the 1980s, a new kind of AI emerged, which was called Machine Learning. Finally, we at least had “Simple Learning.” The big disruption, however, occurred in this decade, when we stumbled upon “Deep Learning,” and it has not been taking prisoners ever since.  
This is, of course, a grossly simplified history of AI. There are actually many different approaches or tribes in AI. In his book, “The Master Algorithm,” Pedro Domingo talks about five different “tribes.” Not to be outdone, A YCombinator user “solidrocketfuel” posts about at least “21 different cultures.”  
It is important for anyone who plans on doing AI to understand there are differences in the approaches of the various AI. AI is not a homogenous field, but rather a field in constant tribal warfare. Here’s an overview:

![](Images/image00173.jpeg)Figure 1.3 Many Tribes of Artificial Intelligence

Symbolists-These are specialists who influence emblematic rule-based frameworks to make inductions. A large portion of AI rotates around this methodology. The methodologies utilizing Lisp and Prolog are in this gathering, as well as the SemanticWeb, RDF, and OWL. One of the most ambitious attempts at this was Doug Lenat’s Cyc which he started back in the ’80s, where he attempted to encode, in logic rules, all that we understand about this world. The significant imperfection was the fragility of this methodology - - individuals generally appear to observe edge situations where their unbending information base doesn’t appear to apply. Reality simply appears to have this sort of fluffiness and vulnerability that is inevitable. It resembles playing an interminable round of Whack-a-mole.

Evolutionists-Folk who apply transformative cycles like crossover and change to show up at developing clever way of behaving. This approach is ordinarily known as Genetic Algorithms (GA). I really do see GA strategies utilized in substitution of an inclination plunge approach in Deep Learning, so this approach doesn’t live in confinement. Folk in this tribe also study cellular automata such as Conway’s Game of Life \[CON\] and Complex Adaptive Systems(CAS). \[CON\]

Bayesians-Folk who utilize probabilistic standards and their conditions to make deductions. Probabilistic Graph Models (PGMs) are a speculation of this methodology and the essential computational system is the Monte-Carlo technique for examining disseminations. The methodology has a few similitudes with the Symbolist approach to such an extent that there is a method for showing up at a clarification of the outcomes. Another benefit of this approach is a proportion of vulnerability communicated in outcomes. Edward is one library that blends this methodology in with Deep Learning.

Kernel Conservatives (Analogizers)- One of the best strategies preceding the strength of Deep Learning was Support Vector Machines (SVM). Yann LeCun refers to this as “celebrated layout coordinating.” There is known as a portion stunt that makes a generally non-straight partition issue into one that is direct. Experts in this field live in charm over the numerical class of their methodology. They accept the Deep Learners are only chemists conjuring up spells without the vaguest comprehension of the consequences.

Tree Huggers —Folk who use tree-based models such as Random Forests and Gradient Boosted Decision Trees. These are basically a tree of rationale rules cutting up the area recursively to fabricate a classifier. This approach has really been really successful in numerous Kaggle contests. Microsoft has a way to deal with merge the tree-based models with Deep Learning.

Connectionists-Folk who accept clever way of behaving emerges from straightforward instruments profoundly interconnected. The principal sign of this was Perceptrons back in 1959. This approach has passed on and was revived a couple of times from that point forward. The most recent manifestation of this is Deep Learning.

There are many sub-approaches under Deep Learning. These methodologies are for the most part extremely free and frequently utilized in mix. The accompanying gatherings presented these approaches:

The Canadian Conspirators-Hinton, LeCun, Bengio et al. Start to finish Deep Learning without manual element engineering.

Swiss Posse-Basically Long Short Term Memory (LSTM) and settled cognizance by utilizing two coordinating Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN). Jurgen Schmidhuber’s gathering in Switzerland did a ton of spearheading work preceding the Deep Learning boom.

Predictive Learners-I utilize the term Yann LeCun evoked to portray unaided learning. This is the cake of AI or the dim matter of AI. This is a significant inexplicable area of AI.

Fuzzy Logicians-This approach was once very famous, yet for reasons unknown, I haven’t heard much about it lately. The differentiation between AI, ML and DL are exceptionally obvious to specialists in these fields. Man-made intelligence is the widely inclusive umbrella covering everything from Good Old Fashion AI (GOFAI) the entire way to connectionist models like Deep Learning. ML is a subfield of AI covering anything to do with the investigation of learning calculations via preparing with information. There are entire areas (not samples) of methods created throughout the long term like Linear Regression, K-implies, Decision Trees, Random Forest, PCA, SVM lastly Artificial Neural Networks (ANN). Counterfeit Neural Networks is where the field of Deep Learning had its genesis.

Some ML specialists with past openness to Artificial Neural Networks (ANN), (after all it was developed in the mid ‘60s), would have the initial feeling that Deep Learning is just ANN with different layers. Besides, the outcome of DL is expected principally to the accessibility of additional information and all the more remarkable computational motors like Graphic Processing Units (GPU). This is, obviously, valid - - the development of DL is basically because of the upsides of having more information and all the more impressive computational systems.

To coin Andreesen  \[WSI\] who said, “Software is eating the world,” “Deep Learning is eating ML.”  
The ongoing DL publicity will in general be that we have calculations which, assuming given an adequate number of information and enough preparation time, can learn all alone. This is obviously either a misrepresentation of what the cutting edge is able to do or a distortion of the genuine act of DL. DL has, throughout the course of recent years, brought about an enormous assortment of thoughts and procedures beforehand either obscure or known to be untenable.  
Deep Learning today goes past staggered perceptrons, yet is rather an assortment of strategies and techniques used to construct composable differentiable designs. These are incredibly skilled AI frameworks and we are presently just seeing a hint of something larger. The vital important point from this is that Deep Learning might seem to be speculative chemistry today, yet we will ultimately figure out how to rehearse it like science. That is, we will have a more strong groundwork empowering us to construct our learning machines with more prominent consistency of its capabilities.  
Armed with this information on the various clans of AI, we can now get a superior comprehension of the improvements in AI. For instance, we can investigate IBM’s strategy.  
In 2011, IBM was large and in charge in the AI field. The organization showed its DeepQA framework had outmaneuvered previous “Peril!” champions. The feat was so impressive, the Jeopardy champion Ken Jennings declared, prior to his imminent loss, “I, for one, welcome our new computer overlords.”  
IBM’s DeepQA utilized numerous AI advancements of now is the ideal time, including Neuro-phonetic Programming (NLP), archive search, information portrayals, surmising motors, and AI. IBM then leveraged the visibility they received to begin a new brand: Watson. Watson is an assortment of a ton of a considerable lot of IBM’s more seasoned items and some fresher ones generally packaged together under the idea of “Mental processing.” Concurrent with these turns of events, Deep Learning started to arise in 2012. On schedule, IBM started integrating Deep Learning in their more up to date Watson Developer Cloud platform.  
Like almost every technology company, IBM found itself underinvested in this new emerging AI approach. IBM, however, was well aware of the value of data, so the company went about acquiring data-rich companies such as Merge Healthcare ($1B), The Weather Company and Truven Health Analytics ($2.6B). In contrast, Intel, which found itself in 2016 to be behind the curve in Deep Learning, rushed out and acquired several Deep Learning startups (i.e. Nervana, Movidius) and in 2017, acquired Mobileye for $15.3B.

### Heart Throbbing Hardware

![](Images/image00174.jpeg)Figure 1.4 Google TPU with its 2D Systolic Array \[PER\]

Google has finally released the technical details of its Tensor Process Unit (TPU) ASIC. Surprisingly, at its core, you find something that sounds like it’s inspired by the heart and not the brain. It’s called a “Systolic Array” and this computational device contains 256 x 256 8bit multiply-add computational units. That’s a grand total of 65,536 processors capable of cranking out 92 trillion operations per second! A systolic array is not a new thing, it was described way back in 1982 by Kung from CMU in “Why Systolic Architectures?” \[KUN\]  And just to get myself dated, I still recall a time when Systolic machines were all the rage.

Here’s a diagram of Google’s TPU:  
![](Images/image00175.jpeg)Figure 1.5 Google’s TPU \[JOU\]

Unlike other computational devices that treats scalar or vectors as primitives, Google’s TPU treats matrices as primitives. The TPU is designed to perform matrix multiplication at a massive scale. If you look at the diagram above, you will notice the device doesn’t have high bandwidth to memory. It uses DDR3 with only 30GB/s to memory. Contrast this to the Nvidia Titan X with GDDR5X hitting transfer speeds of 480GB/s. The systolic array trades off speed for throughput. A Titan X has 3,583 CUDA cores. The CUDA cores are 32bit and are more of general-purpose than 8bit cores of the TPU. Apparently, Google knew way back in 2014 that 8bit was good enough (note: Google had deployed TPU as early as 2015).

Systolic arrays are heavily pipelined, given that it is 256 units wide. It takes 256 cycles from the time the first element gets into the array to the time it comes out and needs twice that many cycles for everything needed to get it to all come out. However, at its peak, you’ll get 65k processors all cranking together. Here’s a slide which shows how a systolic array performs matrix multiplication:

![](Images/image00176.jpeg)

Figure 1.6 Systolic Array performing matrix multiplication  \[SYS\] 

Notice how the matrix elements have to be staggered as they work their way into the array. This lecture by Onur Mutlu \[PER20\] of CMU explains Systolic Arrays:

*Start watching at 1:25:48. A nice coincidence that the speaker uses convolution as an example of a systolic array application.* \[PER20\]   
Another interesting thing about the TPU is that its DDR3 memory seems to be used exclusively for weights. Instructions and data come through the PCIe interface. It surely doesn’t use all of its DDR3 memory for a single DL network. What it may appear to be doing is switching context between different DL networks. You can find some explanation of this design here: “Outrageously Large Neural Network.”  
Anyway, very interesting architecture, unfortunately, it works well only for inference and not for training. However, you just never know if Google has already built something that can also work well for training.  
ASIC development is high risk and expensive proposition. Google had no issue deciding on implementing this in 2014 because, not only did it have the money, they also have a captive user base who would use this. However, this design can easily be copied by the many wanna-be AI chip vendors.  
We could see the same thing happen in the world of Bitcoin. In that field, miners rapidly moved from CPU to GPU, to FPGA and then ASICs. The big leaps in performance occurred in the transitions from CPU to GPU and from FPGA to ASICs. The FPGA route was interesting and served as a good testphase for ASICs but the gains were negligible. So those betting on FPGAs to bring value to Deep Learning (i.e. Xilinx and Intel), should sell their longs and go short. It’ll only be useful for niche and novel use-cases, in short, markets too small to care about.  
The design is going to be replicated because everyone will want a low powered DL component. It doesn’t require high memory bandwidth. It’s a CISC device so the instructions sets are quite compact, requiring an average of 10–20 cycles per instruction, with only a dozen (12) instructions. (Hint: microcode folks, looks like there’s future opportunity here!)About 29% of the die is memory, 24% of it is all 8-bit MACs, so the semiconductor design isn’t rocket science.  
In typical Google fashion, use simple hardware, crack the problem in software, and the secret sauce here is in the software. To pull this off, you have to have an intimate understanding of Deep Learning workloads. How else do you figure out what CISC instructions are important? You also need a bit of compiler design, because apparently the host CPU seems to be doing a lot of prep work to align data and manage results.  
ASIC systolic arrays are going to flood the market with likely Chinesegovernment-subsidized-hardware-manufacturers cranking this stuff out. It just takes someone to come up with an open-source reference design. Just like what happened in the Bitcoin world. This stuff is ancient design stuff.  
Since the original release of Google’s TPU, many new developments followed up. The original Google developer decided to start its own TPU company and raised $10m. \[LER\] Nvidia’s new Volta architecture includes a similar systolic inspired design capable of 120 trillion operations per second. \[DUR\]  Google has its 2nd generation TPU with 180 trillion ops per second and 64GB of ultra high bandwidth ram. \[CLO\]

### The Sputnik Moment in Asia

Many younger readers may be unfamiliar with the history of Sputnik. The Soviet Union’s achievement in launching the first man-made satellite (i.e. Sputnik) in 1957 had an outsized effect on the American psyche. Sputnik created an urgency for America to upgrade its science and technology infrastructure.

*Sputnik also contributed directly to a new emphasis on science and technology in American schools*. With a sense of urgency, Congress enacted the 1958 National Defense Education Act,  \[NDEA\] which provided lowinterest loans for college tuition to students majoring in math and science. After the launch of Sputnik, a poll conducted and published by the University of Michigan showed that 26% of Americans surveyed thought that Russian sciences and engineering were superior to that of the United States.

In March 2016, De epMind’s AlphaGo bested Go’s world champion Lee Sedol. This was viewed by a shocked audience of over 200 million people. A vast majority of the audience came from countries where the game of Go is popularly played (i.e. China, Japan, Korean). The game of Go has a special reverence in China, it’s a 2,500-year-old game traditionally of the four arts \[ART\] that the aristocrats considered as essential accomplishments:

They are *qin* (the guqin \[GQN\], a stringed instrument, 琴\[QIN\]), *qi* (the strategy game of Go \[GGO\], 棋 \[CHR1\]), *shu* (Chinese calligraphy \[CHI\]書 \[CHR2\]) and *hua* (Chinese painting \[PNT\] 畫 \[CHR3\]).

To have Western developed automation arrive and vanquish a legendary player like Lee Sedol certainly shocked many Asian populations to its core. Chinese authorities were concerned enough about the social disruption they hastily imposed a country-wide ban on the live-streaming \[BAN\] of the game. This kind of a shock to one’s core view of the world will likely galvanize a nation into serious action.

The Koreans promptly created an 860 million dollar fund  \[ROK\] right after the game:  
*Korea announced on 17 March that it would invest $863 million (1 trillion won) in artificial-intelligence (AI) research over the next five years. The commitment includes an already-budgeted 138.8 billion won for 2016; if the rest is spread evenly over the following four years, it represents a 55% increase in annual funding for AI.* \[ROK\]  
Not to be surpassed, a July 20th, 2017 article from \[CHN\] NY Times

reported China’s heavy investment on AI:

*Many are spending hundreds of millions of dollars, but some have earmarked even more. In June, the government of Tianjin, an eastern city near Beijing, said it planned to set up a $5 billion fund to support the AI industry.* \[CHN\]

In addition, just look at the heavy investment money flowing into Chinese AI startups.\[AIS\] The funding appears to be 10 times more than what you find for US and European AI startups.

A Nikkei report from August 2017  \[NIK\] titled, “Japan to pump funding into AI chip development” writes about heavy investment by the Japanese of AI hardware:

*To fund the program, the ministry plans to seek more than 10 billion yen from the fiscal 2018 budget, and will also finance basic studies of nextgeneration semiconductors.* \[NIK\]

All the above announcements indicate substantial government funding. The Russians (who don’t usually play Go) don’t have their own Sputnik event to galvanize more heavy investment. However, Vladimir Putin took matters into his own hands by imploring the need to educate his population through a broadcasted speech to the students \[PUT1\] of Russia. He tells Russian students that“the one who becomes the leader in this sphere will be the ruler of the world.” In classic Russian fashion, if they can’t supply their people with arms, then they might as well have them rely on their grit and perseverance. The Russian objectives \[RUSS\] however are quite alarming:  
*The government’s Military Industrial Committee has set a target of making 30 percent of military equipment robotic by 2025.* \[RUSS\]  
In stark contrast to these panicking nations, the EU and US government have been conspicuously absent from announcing any major investments in Artificial Intelligence, and more specifically, in Deep Learning. I won’t hold my breath waiting for this to change. For most Westerners, few have ever played or much less seen a game of Go. DeepMind’s accomplishment is seen as some obscure esoteric achievement requiring very little urgency in response. There is zero appreciation of the magnitude of this achievement.  
I surmise AI wasn’t considered to be a public good to be shared by its citizenry. The West appears to be all perfectly fine surrendering their own privacy to a few private monopolists in exchange for an occasional dopamine fix. A recent article \[SPU\] tells us about the sad state of affairs:  
*To date, the Trump administration has paid little attention to how AI is likely to affect AmericanS—or the world writ large. Treasury Secretary Mnuchin has cavalierly dismissed \[MNUC\] concerns that automation will displace U.S. workers; the Office of Science and Technology Policy lies in shambles \[TRP\]*; *and the State Department’s science envoy recently resigned \[SCI\] while calling for the president’s impeachment.* \[SPU\]  
Well, at least the Canadians are proactively throwing in some spare change \[CND\] to address the rising competition:  
*In Budget 2017, the Government of Canada announced $125 million in funding for a Pan-Canadian Artificial Intelligence (AI) Strategy to be led by the Canadian Institute for Advanced Research (CIFAR).* \[CND\]  
The Severe lack of government subsidy in the U.S. is forcing academic institutions into selling their souls to private corporations. Corporations want to lock-in the intellectual property as fast as possible, and the best way to do that is to lock-up the Deep Learning researchers. Meanwhile academic institutions with the smarts are starved of government research funding and are forced into indentured servitude. The latest MIT-IBM announcement \[MIB\] is simply a reflection of this dire predicament.  
In conclusion, there indeed has been a Sputnik moment for East Asian countries. The consequence of this is an urgency to upgrade its competitiveness in AI and Deep Learning. Meanwhile, the rest of the world is mostly complacent, neglecting vital research funding and remaining unaware of the disruptive potential of this new technology.  
In my opinion, I don’t think the West can rest on the idea that firms like Google, Microsoft and Facebook are performing the majority of the research in Deep Learning. Certainly, we see them sharing some of their research. However, I highly doubt they will be quick to share their biggest breakthroughs. These firms are way ahead of anything the government is doing. A recent report shows the current problems the defense industry \[DEF\] is having:  
*“That’s a hard problem to solve because … a lot of the best minds are moving to these large commercial ventures,” he said. “You can’t even find them in the universities anymore. They’re being stripped out of universities to go work and develop algorithms” in places like Silicon Valley.* \[DEF\]  
To conclude, current AI research and funding will benefit only a few firms and a few nations. For the rest of us, we’ll have to beg others to be kind enough to share their discoveries.

Deep Learning is Eating the World

In this chapter I will discuss applications of Deep Learning in a more general sense. I will cover the ideas of self-driving enterprises, assistive and generative forms of automation, behavior prediction, and a recent advance revelation about how Deep Learning can teach us about complexity. We end the chapter with a list of applications that have been developed using this disruptive technology.

### Self Driving Machines

Self Driving Cars are all the rage these days. When Udacity announced a “self driving car engineering degree” a few months ago, they were swamped with 11,000 hopefuls \[SAG\]:

*The high number of applicants—for 250 spots in the course— underscores the pressing need for talent by technology leaders such as Alphabet’s Google and Apple, traditional car companies and automotive start-ups, as they race to develop production-ready autonomous-driving vehicles within the next decade.* \[SAG\]

Self-Driving Automation is an entirely new field. It is usually described as Self-Driving Cars. Knowledge in this space is in its infancy. It is a complex field involving the integration of many different technologies and the real-time orchestration of these integrations. A glimpse of this idea--automation that employs Deep Learning---Vision, Sensor Fusion and many other technologies, can be found in Amazon Go. Amazon Go isn’t a car---it is a self-service retail store!\[ALB\]

*As for how its “Just Walk Out Shopping” experience works , Amazon seems emphatically not to want to share details. It steeps its description of how the system works in buzzwords: computer vision, sensor fusion, and deep learning. It uses sensors throughout the store and artificial intelligence to tell which direction customers are looking, even in a crowd, and can identify partially blocked labels. Beyond that, details are hazy.* \[ALB\]

The software development field contributed hugely to the way we do work and the way we run businesses. In the early 2000s, the “Agile Manifesto”  \[BEC\] was created in response to our collective inability to effectively manage complex software development. The tenets of the manifesto are as follows:

*Individuals and interactions over processes and tools  
Working software over comprehensive documentation  
Customer collaboration over contract negotiation  
Responding to change over following a plan  
That is, while there is value in the items on the right, we value the items on the left more.* \[BEC\]

Software development is fundamentally a knowledge creation activity. The factory floor processes so effective in the physical world simply don’t apply in the virtual world. The agile manifesto created to challenge our conventional wisdom of processes was prevalent at that time.

Agile development evolved since then and what we see is the practice of employing automation wherever needed. Programmers are extremely lazy folk---rather than perform any kind of repetitive and monotonous work, their preference is to code out automation. It is therefore unsurprising to find so much automation in agile practices. Examples of this include intelligent editors, automated testing, continuous integration and deployment, tracking dashboards, issue trackers that automate tracking, and version control that automate change control. The most sophisticated uses of automation is found in agile software development, and this is indeed a rich source of ideas

regarding how to integrate advanced DL technology into one’s own processes.

The key advantage of agility is its ability to respond quickly to change. If you can automate sections of that feedback loop without, then you have a tighter loop and therefore a more nimble process.

The concepts of “continuous integration” and DevOps are enabled because of automation. In other words, agile processes are effective because there is an unrelenting motivation to insert automation in order to reduce human work. This kind of automation, however, works in synergy with human processes. The correct prescription for introducing automation in one’s processes is to begin with a process centered around humans and then to amplify that with automation.

The agile approach is in contrast with the waterfall approach wherein it begins with a factory floor model and then automates the parts. This leaves you with even more inflexible processes and many unhappy workers. The mechanization of work may have worked in an earlier era lacking the coordination and communication capabilities of computers. However in a world where computation and devices are abundant and pervasive, the only efficient workflow model one should consider is that based on adaptive principles.

Ideas coming from agile development led to further usage in the world of startups. The startup world is one where agility is a critical capability for survival. Startups are buying into the agile philosophy of prioritizing feedback with potential customers. The purpose is to create a learning organization by effectively iterating through many business plans. For startups with a lack of vision, this kind of “The Lean Startup” process is better than having no process at all.  \[LEA\]  How can we learn more quickly what works, and discard what doesn’t? That’s the essence of the approach.

### Artistic Machines

We can group DL abilities into two sorts: Assistive and Generative. These sorts are separated by how a human interfaces with the DL framework. In the two sorts, the human is seldom unaware of what’s going on; the human is somewhat in consistent joint effort with the AI in their work.

We are exceptionally acquainted with mechanization with assistive abilities. We experience it each day when we message on our phones! The telephone can consequently spell accurately and give proposed words before we could complete what we are composing. We additionally observe this capacity in the cameras we use. We currently underestimate the auto-center elements of our cameras. Fresher DL-driven cameras like the ones found in Google’s Pixel can even obscure out the picture’s experiences making a bokeh impact. This was once an ability just accessible on cameras with focal points fit for more prominent gap (or a more modest f-number). Assistive abilities permit us to work at a speedier speed, improving our substance creation on the fly towards significantly more prominent quality.

We likewise see a large number of these assistive advances sent in the product improvement space. IDEs are empowered with numerous capacities helping and amending engineers on the fly. We have style checkers, bug checkers, security weakness checkers, code measurements, code inclusion and others, applied to source code as we compose code. This sort of mechanized criticism through persistent coordination is the best practice in programming improvement. There are instruments that right as well as perform monotonous undertakings requiring accuracy like refactoring. As may be obvious, assistive abilities can occur continuously as well as in the backend. DL permits us to perform dull and tedious undertakings like arranging and sorting our photograph collections.

We are likewise constantly over-burden with data. There are sure callings where the capacity to organize and investigate data is very significant. We can upgrade these curation and investigation abilities by decreasing the downpour of data into more modest lumps all the more rapidly digestible.

### The Uncanny Valley

Let me add a touch of discourse about this turn of events. The goal of the specialists was most certainly not to make a superior sight to behold, but instead to scale the ongoing GAN technique to bigger pictures. The use of this strategy is plainly in its capacity to make reproductions. Making reenactments will have esteem in additional complex prescient issues. The more precise the reproductions can be produced rapidly, the more exact we can make probabilistic models.

The frightening thing Notwithstanding is that these are created pictures. All that CGI can’t match these regarding tricking the mind into trusting it to be genuine. In the Star Wars film “Rebel One” the artists utilized a CGI picture of the late Peter Cushing. The human cerebrum is by all accounts truly adept at perceiving genuine appearances. This is reflected in the ‘uncanny valley’ impact that makes us uncomfortable when we see something that looks near genuinely however too far off. It’s our instinct letting us know something is off.

These DL-produced delivered pictures, notwithstanding, don ’t appear to have that uncanny valley impact. I’m uncertain however in the event that the uncanny valley impact will show moving video. Deep Learning networks are instinct machines, and as an outcome, their capacity to produce what we find in a way that tricks our own instinct, is for sure uncanny.

### Reading Human Minds

One of the most encouraging utilizations of Deep Learning (DL) is its utilization in upgrading communications with PCs. DL is especially appropriate for this since it has a natural ability like organic cerebrums. It can deal with the innate capriciousness and fluffiness of the regular world.

This in all actuality does anyway infer that further developing AI to human connection will require a degree of ability of an AI to anticipate human way of behaving. This is an essential capacity and strangely, there is some new examination on this topic.

The customary conduct model methodology is to have social researchers assemble insightful models (factual models as a rule) of human way of behaving and to utilize a dispersion fitting activity to confirm the legitimacy of the model. The DL approach is anyway unique since an earlier model isn’t expected in the first place. Rather, the DL framework figures out how to anticipate by noticing the way of behaving of human members. We can get a brief look at this weighty procedure by inspecting some new examination work utilizing this approach.

MIT trained a Predictive Vision \[VIS\] system on YouTube videos from shows like “The Office” and “Desperate Housewives” to predict whether two persons will hug, kiss, shake hands or slap a five. \[CSI\] They prepared the Deep Learning framework on 600 hours of video. The framework had the option to anticipate an activity 43 percent of the time. This analyzes to past calculations that main anticipated 36% of the time.

One down to earth utilization of foreseeing human way of behaving is to do as such in the bounds of a vehicle. Brains4Cars uses a sensor fusion Deep Learning system based on LSTMs to anticipate driver behavior 3.5 seconds before it works out. \[BRA\] It uses a collection of sensors such as cameras, tactile sensors and wearable devices to make its predictions.

A recent NIPS 2016 paper uses  Deep Learning to predict strategic behavior \[HWL\]. In many frameworks, the assumption is that the participants perform in a perfectly rational manner and are based on insights from cognitive psychology and experimental economics. In this framework be that as it may, one in light of Deep Learning, the framework learns a mental model without the requirement for master information. This framework can beat frameworks worked from skillfully built features.

Extending past making forecasts, Deep Learning frameworks have been utilized to aid settings with human exchange. In a paper, “Reinforcement Learning of Multi-Issue Negotiation Dialogue Policies” \[NEG\]  the authors used Reinforcement Learning and a handcrafted agenda- based policy and evaluated them by having each negotiate against the other in different settings. \[PAP\] It was found the Reinforcement Learning (RL) model reliably beated the handmade plan based model. Likewise, people were approached to rate the two frameworks and the outcome was, the RL based approach was appraised to be more“rational.”

Finally, in an amazing demonstration of designing, a group from the Czech Republic and Canada made a Poker playing framework that rivaled 33 expert poker players from 17 nations in Heads Up No-Limit Texas hold’em poker. The framework acquired a success rate that was a significant degree better than a decent player rating. The group authored their creation DeepStack. \[MMN\]

DeepStack adopts an in a general sense different strategy. It keeps on utilizing the recursive thinking of CounterFactual Reasoning (CFR) to deal with data imbalance. In any case, it doesn’t figure and store a total methodology preceding playing and hence has no requirement for express reflection. Rather it considers what is going on as it emerges during play, however not in disconnection. It tries not to reason about the whole rest of the game by subbing the calculation past a specific profundity with a quick approximate

gauge . This estimate can be thought of as DeepStack’s intuition \[DEE\]: *a premonition of the benefit of holding any conceivable private cards experiencing the same thing. At long last, DeepStack’s instinct, similar as human instinct, should be prepared. We train it with Deep Learning utilizing models produced from arbitrary poker circumstances. We show that DeepStack is hypothetically solid, creates significantly less exploitable methodologies than deliberation based procedures, and is the main program to beat proficient poker players at HUNL with a noteworthy typical success pace of north of 450 mbb/g.* \[DEE\]

### Natural Stupidity

Do you know what’s more dangerous than artificial intelligence? Natural stupidity. Let’s explore natural stupidity in more detail and show how our current technology (driven by narrow artificial intelligence) is making us collectively dumber.

We’ve all had this experience of using a GPS to guide us around an unfamiliar place only to realize later we have no recollection or ability to get to that place again without the aid of a GPS. Not only is our directional instinct diminished because of lack of use, but so is our own memories. We’ve all experienced losing our ability to recall due to our over use of Google. We now recall more as to how we can search for something rather than the details of that something.

The framework I often use to explore intuition is the Cognitive Bias Codex \[LIS\] found at Wikipedia. It’s a massive list of biases, however to get an overview of it, there are four high level categories that are the drivers of these biases. These are “Too Much Information,” “Not Enough Meaning,” “Need to Act Fast” and “What Should we Remember?”

![](Images/image00185.jpeg)Figure 3.4 Cognitive Bias Codex \[LIS\]

Our world requires more automation to run efficiently and sustainably. The products and services in demand are the products that compensate for our inadequacies. The clear downside of this is that with every assist, the less we exercise our already weak facilities.

The only people maintaining their smarts are the few people willing to constantly exercise their smarts. Meanwhile, we have a population that is becoming more out of shape and lazy with their own mental faculties. We imagine ourselves as being smarter because we can multitask more. Yet, our brains have not evolved to do multi-tasking well. In fact, recent research has shown that pigeons have greater multi-tasking capabilities than humans.

\[PGN\] It is just ironic how we’ve taken pride in our new found multi-tasking skills only to discover we are dumber at it than pigeons!

However, there is a far worse problem than automation making us dumber. The bigger problem is that other humans are aware it can make us dumber and they are opportunistically exploiting our natural stupidity to influence our behaviour. Over the decades, the industry of advertising has spent trillions of dollars inventing new ways to “motivate” us to do new things without us being aware of its influence. The techniques to do this neatly fall under the exploitation of our cognitive biases. After all, if we were indeed all perfectly logical, then we’ll likely spend our money in the most efficient way possible and very few companies will like us to do that. If we reduce our spending, our economies would stall and there would be an economic depression! (BTW, something is really wrong when we must accelerate our consumption so as to avoid economic stagnation.)

So, “Natural Stupidity” is basically our lack of meaning, lack of memory, inability to think fast and inability to process too much information. The current systems we have in place provide products and services to substitute these inabilities. It is the natural tendency to seek out the method of least action. That is, the method that requires the least effort or the laziest thing we can do.

Let’s explore each of the four drivers of cognitive bias in greater detail . Humans, from the beginning of their life, are driven to seek meaning. “The simplest explanations for this are going to be the most naturally appealing ones.” Civilization will naturally create religion to not only create a necessary shared understanding of acceptable behaviour but one driven by

our need for meaning.  
The written word (i.e. books) and it’s more advanced form, the World  
Wide Web are devices addressing our limited memories. Memories require  
not only storage but also the capability of recall. Throughout history, religion  
and law have been transcribed in scrolls, books and now in automation (see:  
blockchain). Money is a form of memory, that is, one’s possession of it is a  
measure of one’s ability to acquire goods and services.  
Mankind created computers to automate the math we invented.  
Computers not only store memories but are able to perform laborious and  
error-free computations. We find it an inconvenience to use cash in that we  
have to calculate in our minds the amount of change so as to guard against  
error or outright fraud. We have time keeping devices so we don’t need to  
look out into the heavens to determine the time of day. We have GPS devices  
to help us avoid reading a map and calculating a path to our destination. Finally, we have the problem of information overload. Our knowledge  
driven economies have accelerated our consumption of information.  
However, our brains have not magically evolved to process this fire-hose of  
information. The devices we use to process more information are services  
that curate information and exhaust it out in more easily digestible forms.  
Today, social networks such as Twitter and Facebook have become our  
primary tools for curating and receiving new information about the world. It is dumbfounding that the leaders of these two companies believe it is not in their charter to ‘police’ the contents they help propagate. With great power  
comes great responsibility, unless I guess, if it affects the bottom line! We collectively become dumber when we relinquish responsibility and  
accountability to the automation (or AI) that furnishes us with cognitive  
assistance.Also when we avoid questioning the positions of our religious  
leaders and ignore obviously repugnant behaviour in defence of our own  
beliefs.We avoid verifying our history and cling to untrue historical  
information to justify our beliefs. This is the case for Neo-Nazis and  
Confederates who would like to imagine a more benevolent and just past.We  
ignore common sense by following algorithms in enforcement of procedures.  
Like the United Airlines where a 70 year old doctor was assaulted and  
removed from an airline just because the crew blindly followed protocol

### Hacking Intuition

On July 28, 2015 a group of AI scientists, led by Max Tegmark, published an open letter concerning the creation of weaponized AI.  
Open Letter on Autonomous Weapons - Future of Life Institute \[OPE\]  
They write the following:  
*The key question for humanity today is whether to start a global AI arms race or to prevent it from starting. If any major military power pushes ahead with AI weapon development, a global arms race is virtually inevitable, and the endpoint of this technological trajectory is obvious: autonomous weapons will become the Kalashnikovs of tomorrow. Unlike nuclear weapons, they require no costly or hard-to-obtain raw materials, so they will become ubiquitous and cheap for all significant military powers to mass-produce. It will only be a matter of time until they appear on the black market and in the hands of terrorists, dictators wishing to better control their populace, warlords wishing to perpetrate ethnic cleansing, etc. Autonomous weapons are ideal for tasks such as assassinations, destabilizing nations, subduing populations and selectively killing a particular ethnic group.* \[OPE\]  
which led to some research on “Concrete Problems in AI Safety.” \[AMO\] Unfortunately, by November 2016 a different kind of weaponized AI arrived with devastating effects: “How We Broke Democracy (But Not in the Way You Think)”: \[ROS\]

*This contact is important in the context of our social channels. They are designed to let us insulate ourselves from the people and opinions we would prefer not to see.* \[ROS\]

In “Blue Feed, Red Feed”  \[KEE\] the author writes:  
*Facebook’s role in providing Americans with political news has never been stronger—or more controversial. Scholars worry that the social network can create “echo chambers,” where users see posts only from like- minded friends and media sources.* \[KEE\]  
These “filter bubbles” or “echo chambers” are prime staging areas for further exploitation. Persuasive Machines are AI driven automation, leveraging knowledge of our cognitive biases to hack into our thought processes, leading to persuasion (or alternatively mind control.)  \[LIS\] An example of this exploit is the rampant rise of fake news and events on Facebook. \[PIC\] Mike Caulfied writes, “Despite Zuckerberg’s Protests, Fake News Does Better on Facebook Than Real News. Here’s Data to Prove It”: \[CAU\]

![](Images/image00186.jpeg)Figure 3.5 Fake news sources \[CAU\]

Fake news are orders of magnitude more popular than real news. And it is not just fake news that’s a problem, security breaches are also another. Maria Korolov writes: \[KOR\]

*“It’s a combination of a lot of things that we’ve seen for a lot of years coming together,” said Ric Messier, head of the cybersecurity program at Burlington, Vt.-based Champlain College.* \[CHP\] *“The fact that it’s so easy to do this leaking and be able to manipulate people in this way certainly suggests that we’re probably just starting to see the beginning of these sorts of activities or attacks.”* \[KOR\]

Automation in the form of Twitter bots have amplified messaging to bring about a ‘bandwagon effect’ to influence the masses. In “The Algorithmic Democracy”: \[CAM\]

*Analyzing Twitter during three televised debates, they discovered that 20% of all political tweets were made by bots. … These bots, they wrote, can make online conversations more polarized. They make it easier to spread factually incorrect news stories. These automatons, in combination with massive hacking and phishing attacks by a nation state were part of a coordinated effort to undermine the decision making of the population. \[SZO\] Esquire magazine has an even more detailed account of this in “How Russia Pulled the Greatest Election Hack in History.” \[RID\]

Jonathan Albright has an even more detailed analysis of what he calls: “Micro Propaganda Machines.” \[ALBR\] Albright writes:

These sites have created an ecosystem of real-time propaganda: they include “viral” hoax engines that can instantly shape public opinion through mass “reaction” to serious political topics and news events. This network is triggered on-demand to spread false, hyper-biased, and politically-loaded information.* \[ALBR\]  
Scott Adams (of Dilbert fame) understands the power of persuasion quite well in “No One Understands Donald Trump Like the Horny Narcissist Who Created Dilbert”: \[DOL\]  
*Adams, like Trump, recognized that the election would play out at the limbic level of primal furies and genital anxieties.* \[DOL\]

Win the AI race.

Not a very promising list of options. The threat of AI is not some futuristic idea; it is indeed here today and will become ever more sophisticated. In a later chapter, we will further discuss this dimension of artificial social intelligence.

### Human Legacy Bias

Humans are very good at talking themselves out of investing heavily on new and disruptive technologies. Here are some of the reasons experts around you will use to talk you out of investing in Deep Learning.

**It’s just Machine Learning**  
Practitioners’ introduction to neural networks is usually via the introduction of linear regression which then moves to logistic regression. That’s because the mathematical equations for an artificial neural network (ANN) are identical. Therefore, immediately there is a bias that the characteristics of these classical Machine Learning (ML) methods will also convey into the world of DL. After all, DL is, in its most naive explanation, nothing more than multiple layers of ANN.  
There are also other kinds of ML methods with equations different from DL. The basic objective for all ML methods is, however, a general notion of curve fitting. That is, if the data is a good fit with a model, then perhaps, that is a good solution. Unfortunately, with DL systems, these systems will by default over-fit any data, because the number of parameters in the model is so large. This is enough of an indication that a DL is an entirely different kind of animal from an ML system.  
**It is just Optimization**  
DL systems have a loss function that is a measure of how well its predictions match its input data. Classic optimization problems also have loss functions (also known as objective functions). In both systems, different kinds of heuristics are used to discover an optimal point in a large configuration space. It was once thought that the solution surface of a DL system was sufficiently complex so that it would be impossible to arrive at a solution. Curiously enough however, one of the simplest methods of optimization, the Stochastic Gradient Descent algorithm, is all that is needed to arrive at surprising results.  
What this tells you is, there is something else going on here that is actually very different from what optimization folks are used to.  
**It’s a Black Box**  
Many Data Scientists have an aversion to DL because of the lack of interpretability of its predictions. This is a characteristic of not only DL methods, but classical ML methods as well. Data Scientists would rather use Probabilistic methods where they can have better control of the models or priors. As a result, they have systems capable of making predictions with the least number of parameters. All are driven by the belief that parsimony, or Occam’s razor, is the optimal explanation for everything.  
Unfortunately, probabilistic methods are not competitive in classifying images, speech, or even text. That’s because DL methods are superior to human beings in discovering models. Brute force just happens to trump wetware. There’s no experimental evidence in the DL space that parsimonious models work any better than entangled models. For those cases where it is an absolute requirement to have some kind of explanation, there are now newer methods in DL providing aid to interpretability, as well as uncertainty. If a DL system can generate the captions in an image, then there is a good chance it can be trained to generate an explanation for a prediction.  
**It’s too early and too soon**  
There is a natural bias that something that was around five years old and rapidly evolving is too new and volatile a technology to trust. I think we all said the same thing when the microprocessor, Internet, Web and mobile technologies came along. Wait-and-see was the safe approach for almost everyone and this was certainly a reasonable approach for anyone who has not really spent the time investigating the details. However, it is a very risky strategy, ignorance may be bliss, but another company eating your lunch can mean extinction.  
**There is too much hype**  
There are a lot of things DL can do that were deemed inconceivable just a few years ago. Nobody expected a computer to beat the best human player in Go. Nobody expected self-driving cars to exist today. Nobody expected to see Star Trek universal translator-like capabilities. It is so unbelievable, it is more likely to be an exaggeration, rather than something that may be real. I hate to burst your bubble of ignorance, but DL is in fact very real, and you experience its benefits when you use any smartphone.  
**AI winter will likely come again**  
We’ve had so many times where the promise of AI led to disappointing results. And the argument goes further than that because its failure has happened so often before, and is bound to happen again. The problem with this argument is, despite disappointment, AI research has led to many software capabilities we take for granted today and thus never notice its existence. Good, old-fashioned AI (GOFAI) is embedded in many systems today.  
The current pace of DL development is accelerating and there are certainly big problems that need to be solved. The need for more training data and the lack of unsupervised training are two problems. This however doesn’t  
mean that what we have today has no value. DL can already drive cars. That in itself tells you that even if another AI winter arrives, we would have achieved a state of development still quite useful.  
**There is not enough theory of how it works**  
The research community does not have a solid theoretical understanding as to why DL works so effectively. We have some idea as to why a multilayer neural network is more efficient in fitting functions than one with fewer layers. However, we don’t have an understanding as to why convergence even occurs, or why good generalization happens. At this time, DL is very experimental and we are just learning to characterize these kinds of systems. Meanwhile, despite not having a good theoretical understanding, the engineering barrels forward. Researchers, using their intuition and educated guesses, are able to build exceedingly better models. In other words, nobody is stopping their work to wait for a better theory. It is almost analogous with what is happening in biotechnology research. People are experimenting with many different combinations and arriving at new discoveries they have yet to explain. Scientific and technological progress is very messy and one shouldn’t shy away from the benefits because of the chaos.  
**It is not biologically inspired**  
DL systems are very unlike the neurons in our brain. The mechanism of how DL learns (i.e. SGD) is not something we can explain using what happens in our brain. The argument here though, is that if it doesn’t resemble the brain then it is unlikely to be able to perform the kind of inference and learning of a brain. This is of course an extremely weak argument. After all, planes don’t look like birds, but they can certainly fly.  
**I’m not an expert in it**  
Not having inhouse expertise shouldn’t be an excuse for not finding expertise outside. It also should not prevent you from having your experts learn this new technology. If these experts, however, have the dogmatic persuasion, then that should be an indication you should get a second and unbiased opinion.  
**It does not apply to my problems**  
Businesses are composed of many business processes. Unless you have gone through the exercise of examining the processes that can be automated with current DL technologies, you are not in a position to make the statement that DL does not apply to you. Furthermore, you may discover new processes and business opportunities that may not exist today, but are possible with the double-dealing of DL innovation. You can’t actually respond to this inquiry until you have put resources into some expected level of effort work.  
**I don’t have the resources**  
The huge Internet organizations like Google and Facebook have eaten up a ton of the Deep Learning ability out there. These organizations have almost no interest in working with a private company to recognize their particular requirements and open doors. Luckily, these enormous organizations have been sufficiently generous to permit their scientists to distribute their work. We really do subsequently have a perspective on their most recent turns of events and are consequently ready to take what they’ve realized and apply it to our context.

### A Reality Checklist

Where is Deep Learning material? This is one of the additional temporary plans to comprehend about Deep Learning and related AI advances. It is really quite simple to fall into the snare that an “Man-made reasoning” application can tackle your problem.

The standard inclusion of this issue includes the subject of “do you have an adequate number of information?” Unfortunately, that is excessively ambiguous; to answer this you need to at minimum get your concern space. In the scholarly sense, you need to comprehend the “limit conditions.” Said in an unexpected way, you need to get the characteristic imperatives of your concern. What precisely are “limit conditions” of Deep Learning or AI learning problem?

To comprehend an issue area, you need to get what is the present status of information. Vulnerability is a proportion of what is obscure comparative with what is comprehensible. The peculiar supposition of the word vulnerability is, it accepts everything can ultimately be known. We expect determinism, yet in reality, this is seldom ever the case.

Nevertheless, it is a valuable term we will use to distinguish the limits of an issue. Here is the agenda, written as questions, and a response of “no” suggests the presence of vulnerability. Oddly enough, I can’t express it one more method for simplifying it! Contrapositive statements  
consistently appear to be muddled to parse! I will likewise utilize the terms entertainers, action and climate to depict the whole context.

Execution vulnerability Does the grouping of the activities of the entertainers, from the climate’s underlying state, consistently lead to a similar last state?

Observational vulnerability Do entertainers have total data of the environment?  
Duration vulnerability Do the entertainers know how long the movement will last?  
Action vulnerability Are the impacts of the entertainer’s activities known exactly?  
Evaluation vulnerability Is there an assessment rules to gauge the fruitful finish of the activity?  
Training vulnerability Is there information or information of past effective arrangements that can be utilized as direction to learning?  
In most real world problems, the answers to these questions are more likely to be negative. Automation requires that a majority of the above be answered in the affirmative. Deep Learning based automation however equips the practitioner with a little more wiggle room. The nature of Deep Learning networks is that they are approximation machines. However, DL can only learn a good approximation of an uncertain situation if there exists information that already removes the uncertainty (i.e. supervised learning).  
No machine can know the unknowable (i.e. the unsupervised learning problem). More specifically, systems learn by using knowable knowns to increase certainty of knowable unknowns. The checklist above guides one to identify what is unknown and thus is a list of “known unknowns.” Let’s be real here, you can’t solve a hard problem if you don’t know what you don’t know! Strive to break that “reality distortion field” by identifying exactly what not is only uncertain but also what is unknowable in your problem domain.  
Deep Learning can’t do wizardry regardless of whether it is drilled like speculative chemistry. Notwithstanding, getting the fluffy limits of what is reality and what is sci-fi can give you an enormous decisive advantage over the competition.

There is an inborn predisposition in the examination local area to request the instruments utilized by Deep Learning to adjust to more inflexible numerical thinking. There is a ton of accentuation in our models which request some probabilistic understanding. I am anyway on the camp supporting that complicated way of behaving arises out of oversimplified systems. There is still a ton of work should have been done here, nonetheless, we can perceive how our momentum models appear to be breaking under the type of a ton of unexplainable observations.

**5\. Links to Human Cognition**

Deep Learning improvements are not even close to tending to what Voss ’ portrays as “vagueness, unique conceptualization and thinking, transient memory and setting, as well as meta-cognition.”

**6\. Exploratory Research**

Fundamental exploration on the way of behaving of Deep Learning takes a secondary lounge to novel methods yielding noteworthy outcomes in limited spaces. It will probably remain this way for some time. The ‘consideration’ market today orders a higher premium on curiosity and benchmarks than major insight.

Note that these are six suspicions that “were generally taken on during the AI’s initial thirty years.” The excellence of this is, we can differentiate what we know today about Deep Learning and perceive how it fits with many years old viewpoint of what should be developed.
